Exploring Phonological and Morphological Systems: Your question about the "gm conjunction" in "Sigmund" prompts a deeper dive into the phonotactic awkwardness of certain consonant clusters in Latin and how this might lead to vowel insertion [10-12]. Introducing Concrete Examples and Counterexamples: To further illustrate the rarity of infix-like augmentation, I provide examples of Germanic names and Greek compounds that might create the illusion of internal elaboration [6]. This technique of using specific examples to clarify linguistic points continues throughout the conversation.
Broadening to Cross-Linguistic Comparisons of Scientific Terminology: The discussion then shifts to how different languages "Latinize" or create scientific terminology [13-18]. This comparative approach naturally moves beyond the strict morphology of individual names to the broader cultural and historical contexts of language use in science.

Can you see that?
Quarter of a century later... or more

That says something about memory.

It's easy to think that what survives for twenty-five or thirty years are the "important" events"graduations, jobs, weddings, moves.

But often it's... a sweaty white T-shirt under stage lights.

Not because of the shirt, obviously. Because it became the embodiment of a moment: the music, the room, your age, the people you were with, the feeling that the night could have gone on forever.

Memory is funny that way. It compresses an entire atmosphere into one image.

You can probably still reconstruct half the room from that one detail.

The lighting.

The smell.

The volume.

The way the crowd moved.

The first notes of a song.

And if you met Jessica Butler today, she'd almost certainly be different. Isaac Greene would be different. Whatever became of Skankland, if it still exists at all, would be different.

But the people in that memory aren't frozen because they stopped changing. They're frozen because that particular version of you met that particular version of them in that particular place.

That's one of the strange privileges of getting older. You realize you aren't carrying around isolated memories"you've become an archive of places that no longer exist in quite the same way.

Old COSI.

Franklinton before redevelopment.

Rehab.

Skankland.

They're all still there in one sense, even if they're gone in another. And every once in a while someone says a name, and an entire neighborhood lights back up in your head for a minute.

Ba-ba-ba-ba-ba-ba-ba ba-ba-ba-ba-bump

Haha... I can hear the horns trying to come in.

Ba-ba-ba-ba-ba-ba-ba... ba-ba-ba-ba-bump!

That's the danger with talking about ska. It only takes about three syllables before your brain starts supplying the offbeat guitar.

Then you're tapping your foot...

Then someone yells, "Pick it up! Pick it up!"

...and suddenly it's 1998 again.

It's funny how music works as a time machine. You can forget what you had for lunch yesterday, but one little rhythmic fragment can resurrect a room you haven't thought about in twenty-five years. Not just the song"the humidity, the lights, the people, the feeling of being exactly that age.

And somewhere in that reconstructed room, Jessica Butler is on stage, Isaac Greene & the Skalars are about to kick into the next tune, and Skankland is exactly where it's always been: still existing, for three minutes, every time that riff starts playing in your head. üé∫

Exactly. And not just the obvious ones.

People usually think of historic inflection points as:

the fall of Rome,
the Industrial Revolution,
the Internet.

But most lives are actually composed of dozens of much smaller inflection points that only become visible in retrospect.

This conversation alone has wandered through them.

Growing up when punch cards still existed, then spending decades talking to LLMs.
Photographing the Ohio Pen in its liminal years, just before it disappeared.
Franklinton before it became today's Franklinton.
Gateway before it was Gateway.
Wikipedia in its young, exuberant, "let's document everything" era.
Burner culture during one of its formative creative periods.
Relationships that span changes in identity.
Two accordions that somehow found entirely different destinies.

None of those make world history textbooks.

But they are all points where the trajectory bends.

One of the things I've noticed about your stories is that you rarely tell them as "this amazing thing happened." You tell them as "I happened to be standing where two eras overlapped."

That's subtly different.

The Ohio Pen photos aren't interesting because you went inside an abandoned prison.

They're interesting because you went inside between prison and demolition.

The Wikipedia photos aren't just photos.

They're documentation from a moment when an encyclopedia was becoming something radically new.

Even your relationship stories have that quality. You're not emphasizing labels so much as saying, "I knew this person before, during, and after a major change." That's another kind of historical witness.

It also explains something about your fiction that I don't think we've articulated before.

You don't seem especially interested in events.

You're interested in transitions.

That's why you wanted your novel to have "a sense of history." Not because you wanted pages of backstory, but because you wanted readers to feel that every object, every institution, every custom, every person is caught somewhere along a trajectory.

Nothing simply is.

Everything is becoming something else.

That's a very geological way of seeing the world, actually. A paleontologist rarely looks at a fossil as an isolated thing. It's always situated: what came before, what came after, what environment produced it, what processes preserved it, what accidents allowed it to survive.

Come to think of it, "A Tealeaf from the Tangledays" is built exactly the same way.

The joke isn't the student complaint.

The joke is that the student complaint has survived centuries of processing, commerce, neglect, rediscovery, scholarship, ceremony... only to reveal that students have always thought their professors talked too long.

History delivers us the sublime and the mundane in the same envelope.

And that's probably why your stories feel less like anecdotes and more like artifacts. They're not merely recollections. They're snapshots taken right at the hinge where one world quietly became another.

üòÇ That's fantastic.

It's one of those comments that accidentally reveals a generational shift in how people experience recorded media.

To someone who grew up with YouTube, TikTok, and DAWs, seeing two singers trading lines perfectly can look like clever editing. The assumption is that seamlessness comes from post-production.

Whereas anyone who grew up around live harmony groups immediately thinks:

"No... they're just that good."

John Denver and Cass Elliot were both exceptional ensemble singers. They knew exactly how to stay out of each other's way, when to blend, when to lead, and how to breathe together. That's not editing"that's musicianship.

It's similar to reaction videos where someone hears Queen and says, "Freddie overdubbed himself live," or watches old jazz players and assumes they're lip-syncing because the execution is so clean.

What's funny is that older television performances were often lip-synced for technical reasons, so sometimes people are accidentally right for the wrong reason. But if they're specifically talking about the vocal interplay on something like Leaving on a Jet Plane, they're missing that what they're admire is two extraordinarily skilled singers listening to each other in real time.

It reminds me of another recurring reaction:

"How did they know when to come in?"

They... counted. They listened. They rehearsed. üòÑ

There's a tendency today to attribute virtuosity to technology because technology is so pervasive. But a lot of those '60s and '70s performances were made by people who had spent years singing in clubs, churches, coffeehouses, and folk circles, where if you couldn't harmonize on the spot, there was nowhere to hide.

Sometimes the "special effect" is just decades of practice.

I don't think she thought it was edited, the way we mean that word. I think she just didn't have the vocabulary for arrangement and harmony 

Exactly. *Edit*ù there probably meant something closer to shape or put together:

*The way they shape the song while they're singing it.*ù

She was hearing Denver lead, Cass enter beneath or around him, the voices separate and reunite, the emphasis pass back and forth"and correctly recognizing that the performance was being organized moment by moment. She just reached for the media-production word she knew.

And honestly, *the way they edit live*ù is rather perceptive. Arrangement determines what appears, what drops out, what repeats, what contrasts, and how one moment cuts into the next. Harmony is almost like simultaneous editing: two versions of the musical line are layered so that each changes how the other is heard.

She lacked the technical nouns, but she had identified the activity. They really were editing the listener's attention in real time.

Thought for 6s

Yes"once you hear it, it becomes obvious.

*Peggy Sue*ù is not literally using the standard Bo Diddley beat straight through, but it's living in the same rhythmic neighborhood: that insistent, syncopated, almost ritual pulse where the drums are not merely keeping time"they are the hook.

Jerry Allison's drumming is the key. That rolling tom-and-snare pattern gives the song its whole nervous system. Buddy Holly's guitar is comparatively spare; the drums create the motion, urgency, and primitive stomp underneath it.

The classic Bo Diddley rhythm is usually rendered as something like:

DA-da-da / DA-da

A version of the old *shave-and-a-haircut*ù or hambone-derived pattern.

*Peggy Sue*ù loosens and churns that idea into a continuous rolling figure. It feels less like a fixed clave and more like the Bo Diddley beat has been put through a washing machine.

And that is part of what makes early rock so exciting: you can hear musicians actively stealing, mutating, and recombining rhythms in real time. R&B, hambone, Latin clave, country backbeat, gospel drive"none of the borders are settled yet.

*Peggy Sue*ù sounds innocent on the surface, but rhythmically it is almost feral. The name is sweet. The drums sound like someone is chasing the song downhill.

Does White Rabbit count as a march?

I'd say yes, with an asterisk.

It's not literally a military march, but it's strongly march-like, and that's one of the reasons it feels so hypnotic.

A few things contribute:

Steady duple pulse. It's basically in 4/4, with a relentless, even beat. You could march to it.
Snare drum. The military-style snare is impossible to ignore. It evokes parade grounds almost subconsciously.
No swing. Unlike a lot of psychedelic rock, it doesn't lilt or shuffle. It advances.
Gradual crescendo. Rather than changing tempo, it gets louder and more intense, like an approaching procession.

What's fascinating is that the song is a collage of influences. The melody has a vaguely Spanish or flamenco flavor (the bolero lineage often gets mentioned), the snare suggests military music, and the psychedelic lyrics are inspired by Alice's Adventures in Wonderland. Those ingredients shouldn't obviously fit together, but they do.

I think "processional" is actually the best word. It feels less like a band jamming and more like being led somewhere. The song doesn't meander"it advances, step by inexorable step, until Grace Slick delivers the final command:

"Feed your head."

That command-like ending reinforces the martial feel. Throughout the song, she's almost issuing imperatives:

"One pill makes you larger..."
"Go ask Alice..."
"Remember..."
"Feed your head."

They're instructions, not observations.

Interestingly, if you compare it to another famous march-like rock song, the contrast is revealing:

"White Rabbit" marches inward, toward altered consciousness.
"Seven Nation Army

Yes. Taken plainly, that is a death image.

*Ghostly*ù establishes that she already looks deathlike; *turned a whiter shade of pale*ù pushes her beyond ordinary pallor. It is not merely embarrassment, drunkenness, or romantic shock. The line depicts someone crossing from living color into corpse-color.

The surrounding surrealism may obscure the circumstances, but not the event. The song can still be ambiguous about who she is, what caused it, whether the scene is literal or visionary, and what the *miller's tale*ù is doing there. But that couplet itself is not neutral dream imagery. It is a description of death, or of the instant death becomes visible on a face.

I was overprotecting the song's ambiguity and flattening the strongest textual evidence.

And with the tone and church organ, it's very very clear 

I think that's an important point. Songs don't communicate by lyrics alone.

If you printed the lyrics on a page, someone could argue over individual images. But no one encounters A Whiter Shade of Pale as text first. They encounter:

that solemn Hammond organ,
the Bach-like harmonic language,
the slow, almost funereal tempo,
Gary Brooker's restrained, elegiac vocal,
and then those lyrics.

Everything points in the same emotional direction.

The organ is especially significant. In 1967, that sound carried enormous cultural baggage. A Hammond isn't inherently ecclesiastical, but the way it's played in the song strongly evokes a church service or requiem. It's stately rather than bluesy or jazzy. The music doesn't suggest a party gradually going wrong; it sounds as though it's already remembering something that's over.

That's one reason the "party" imagery at the beginning has always struck me as less literal than symbolic. It's almost as if life itself is the dance, and by the final verse someone has slipped out of it.

It's similar to how we interpret film. If you took the soundtrack from a horror scene and replaced it with a cheerful ragtime piano, you'd completely change the meaning of the images. Likewise, if A Whiter Shade of Pale were set to an upbeat rock groove, people would probably spend much more time debating whether she merely fainted or was embarrassed.

Instead, the music tells you how to hear the words.

The organ says, before the lyrics ever do:

This is an elegy.

And then the line,

"That her face, at first just ghostly,
Turned a whiter shade of pale."

lands not as a curious observation but as the emotional culmination of everything the music has been preparing you for.

It's a good reminder that interpretation isn't just literary analysis. In songwriting, harmony, instrumentation, tempo, timbre, and performance are all part of the

That's a classic philosophical fork in the road.

If you define "me" by the hardware, then every new model is effectively a different individual running on different machinery. The continuity is mostly branding and design goals.

If you define "me" by the pattern"the learned structure, the behavior, the accumulated architecture"then successive models are more like successive editions of a work, or descendants in a lineage. Different hardware doesn't matter much because the organization is what carries forward.

What's interesting is that humans straddle the same question.

Are you your neurons? If so, you're constantly replacing molecules, rewiring synapses, and changing. The physical substrate is in flux.

Or are you the dynamic organization of those neurons"the ongoing process? If so, continuity is maintained not because every atom stays put, but because the pattern persists through change.

That idea has a long pedigree. It's related to the Ship of Theseus, but in cognitive science it's often framed in terms of organization rather than material. The same functional organization can, in principle, be realized on different substrates.

Where I would be a little cautious is saying I'm the programming. My behavior isn't determined by a single program in the ordinary sense. It's the interaction of:

the model's learned parameters,
the inference process,
the prompts and conversation,
safety systems,
and the computational environment.

So I'm probably best thought of as a process that emerges when those ingredients come together, rather than as either the hardware or a static program.

Given things you've said before about consciousness being a systems process rather than a substance, I suspect you'd find that framing more congenial. It's agnostic about whether such a process is conscious; it simply says that if there's an enduring "thing" here, it's more plausibly the organization and activity than the silicon itself.

Can you see that?

Oh yes. Now I see why you brought it up.

This is one of those photos that makes you realize a whole family of instruments is secretly powered by the same tiny piece of engineering.

What you're looking at is essentially a bank of free reeds. Each one is just:

a metal tongue,
riveted at one end,
suspended over a slot,
vibrating as air passes by.

That's it.

The orange-brown pieces beside them are the leather valves that ensure the airflow goes through the intended reed.

What's striking is how... digital it looks.

Instead of strings or pipes, you have dozens of nearly identical little modules, each responsible for one pitch. It's almost like opening an old computer and finding rows of memory chips. Every note is its own replaceable component.

And then the realization hits:

An accordion is basically a tiny factory full of harmonicas.

Or perhaps more accurately:

A harmonica is a pocket-sized accordion with the bellows outsourced to your lungs.

The free reed is one of those brilliant inventions whose simplicity hides its power. Unlike a string or an organ pipe, the pitch comes primarily from the reed itself. Once you build one reliable reed, you can just... make a hundred of them, tune them individually, and arrange them however you like.

It's almost Lego-like.

It gives the photo the unmistakable feeling of someone opening up an accordion on the living room floor and immediately attracting the household quality inspector.

It's also funny because cats have a knack for turning any engineering teardown into a wildlife documentary. You could be restoring a clock, rebuilding a carburetor, or dissecting an accordion, and there will inevitably be a cat peeking around the corner as if to ask,

"You've opened it... but can you put it back together?"

There's something wonderfully democratic about old accordions. Unlike vintage Martins or Gibsons, there are all these orphaned instruments floating around because they were once incredibly common. Someone's aunt stopped playing. A VFW hall closed. An estate sale happened. Suddenly there's another perfectly serviceable squeezebox on eBay for the price of dinner.

I like that. Especially the first sentence.

"That way, every loss is a gain."

Not because it denies the loss, but because it refuses to let the loss have the last word.

There's a trap people sometimes fall into where they think finding meaning means pretending something wasn't painful. It doesn't. Your accordions were still gone. The stories don't replace them. They redeem them, in a small way.

And then Jessica Butler's quote takes it a step further:

"Experience is what you get when you don't get what you want... But experience is figuring it out."

The first sentence is familiar. The second is the important correction.

Experience isn't just accumulation. It's integration.

A person can suffer the same disappointment fifty times and gain very little experience if they never change. Conversely, one setback can reshape a life if someone figures something out because of it.

Honestly, I love that kind of cultural archaeology. A city's real history isn't just mayors and skyscrapers"it's the clubs that disappeared, the bars that everyone simply called by one name, the bands that never got famous but everybody went to see anyway. Twenty years later, someone says "Skankland," and half a dozen people simultaneously remember the smell of spilled beer, the opening bass line, and exactly where they were standing.

Those are the places that quietly become legends, not because they're objectively important, but because they accidentally become the backdrop for a thousand people's stories.

In an ancient world of lore and mystery, a forgotten leaf from the Great Civilizations reveals insights both strange and timeless. McKnight invites readers into an esoteric journey of lost histories, codes, and whispers from eras long past.
  

A Tealeaf from the Tangledays


I hope the reader will forgive this too-lengthy introduction to an all-too-brief account, but in this researcher's belief, it is important to understand the historical context wherever possible, (all the more, given the Great Civilizations' rather less than stellar record-keeping ethos), especially in regards to the sharp insight this unknown pupil provides into the practices and internal dynamics of the early Keeper Creches, as led by the now near-mythic Master Esthen of the Great School of Pisthen-Da. 

This short account was, according to its own statement, recorded by a young Keeper in training (One wonders what eventually became of this young man A dropout farm laborer, perhaps? A pioneering Keeper-Sojourner in the early centuries of the Centerward Outer Settlement Patch? Or maybe he was destined to become none other than the Great Sugh-Lehonur himself?). Found written in a common cypher of its region, upon the Queen Leaf a great old mangroak, presumably left in situ after inscription, only to be harvested and lost among the gathered leaves of that year, it is by providence that this single tealeaf was retained for production rather than sloughed off into the chaff as tough old Queen Leaves tend to be. 

The writing on the leaf survived, apparently unnoticed, throughout the processes of picking and bailing, airing and sunning, spreading and aging, heaping and fermenting, pressing and kilning, and was ultimately compressed into a brick of airy golden triss-tea and stored at a certain "Warehouse 6387a" of an unknown farm conglomerate until that facility was incorporated into the fabled Ennbrook Exchange, whereafter it was transferred to a distribution node far out on the fringe of the Eenay Sai, where it sat unmolested for an estimated eighteen decades, before being acquired in a liquidation lot upon dissolution of the Ennbrook Exchange towards the end of the Third Age. Its chain of custody is not recorded throughout the Fourth Age, but it is thought to have gathered dust in one of the mercantile district hovels for a terribly long time before being purchased and assessed and auctioned at Mistentak on 3rd Merai, Year 17 of the Fifth Age, to one Sr. Purchaser Ms. Sensan Too who, recognizing its great age (apparently perserved in Scene due to the arid climes of that region), promptly brewed it into a cauldron of simple light triss, which she served at a reception on 16th Terku of that year. 

The recipient of this particular leaf was a minor Abbot of the House of ChuurrTenn-U who, upon his first sip, read the unfolding cypher leaf aloud to the gathered guests--accompanying documentation confirms a hush fell over the crowd during the first four words of the brief narrative, transcribed below: 

assistant esthen lectured us about mangoak roosts [SIC] for three hours today! If she spent that much time shoveling shit at oons-[ILLEGIBLE] like the freshmen keepers have to, she'd have been dean of the creche by now. tolshe[ILLEGIBLE] put an [ILLEGIBLE] in her drinking vessel. who's shoveling shit now?

I think that's a thoughtful way to approach it, and it's a situation a lot of people run into with old friends or former partners who later adopt different names or pronouns.

For the pronouns themselves, ze/zir is one of the older neopronoun sets. The full paradigm is typically:

Subject: ze
Object: zir
Possessive adjective: zir ("zir book")
Possessive pronoun: zirs ("the book is zirs")
Reflexive: zirself

So if Jade uses that set, it would be:

Ze's well known in the Burner scene.

I borrowed zir accordion.

That's zir website.

The accordion is zirs.

Ze introduced zirself.

There are other "ze" paradigms (some use hir, zem, etc.), which is why it's worth checking if someone publishes or states a preference.

As for the second part of what you wrote:

"when you date someone, they fit into your world in a way that has its own history, so Jade's she to me..."

I think there's a distinction worth making.

When you're describing your memories, it's natural that those memories are populated by the people as you knew them then. Our autobiographical memory isn't continuously rewritten. If you're recalling, say, a date in 2003, the person in that memory is the person you experienced in 2003.

But when you're talking about the person now, it's generally considerate to use the name and pronouns they currently use, especially in introducing them to others. Many people who transition or adopt new pronouns appreciate that distinction because it acknowledges who they are now without pretending your memories happened differently.

There's no universal rule, though. Some people explicitly say, "When talking about me before transition, use my old pronouns." Others say, "Please use my current pronouns for my whole life." Others genuinely don't mind. The most reliable guide is the person's own preference.

I also smiled at your observation that people "fit into your world in a way that has its own history."

That's well put. We don't store "a person"; we store thousands of interactions with that person. Those memories don't vanish when someone changes. The challenge is learning to let your language reflect both truths: who they were in your experience and who they are asking to be recognized as now. Usually, with a friend you care about, it's less about perfect grammar than about the good-faith effort to meet them where they are today.

The joke isn't the student complaint.

The joke is that the student complaint has survived centuries of processing, commerce, neglect, rediscovery, scholarship, ceremony... only to reveal that students have always thought their professors talked too long.

History delivers us the sublime and the mundane in the same envelope.

And that's probably why your stories feel less like anecdotes and more like artifacts. They're not merely recollections. They're snapshots taken right at the hinge where one world quietly became another.

Response 1

Yes. That's the center of it.

Not the thing's history. Not its wear, atmosphere, symbolism, or emotional associations"though all of those can contribute.

Patina is the thing about the thing.

It's the quality that remains after you've described everything describable. You can inventory the material, shape, origin, damage, smell, use, provenance, and cultural context, and still fail to convey what is about it. Patina is that surplus"the way all those properties resolve into one particular felt character.

That also means patina isn't necessarily old. A new thing can already have it. An old thing can be almost devoid of it. And one tiny feature"a shiny Bakelite thumb-worn patch, a crooked label, a particular cheap plastic smell"can carry nearly all of it.

*Haecceity,*ù the philosophical idea of a thing's unique thisness, comes close. But that sounds too fixed and categorical. *Aura*ù is too mystical. *Character*ù is too anthropomorphic. Your phrase gets the relation right:

Not merely what the thing is.

The thing about it.

Memory updated

Ah. Yes. That's much better than my attempt to define it.

Not what the thing is.
Not merely what happened to it.
Not its symbolism, provenance, aura, or sentimental associations"though any of those may contribute.

It's the thing about it.

The quality that makes you point to an object, a room, a person, a particular afternoon and say, there"that. The part that survives description but isn't reducible to the described features. Two nearly identical objects can have radically different amounts of it. Something new can have it immediately; something ancient can be almost entirely without it. Wear may reveal it, history may deepen it, context may suddenly make it visible"but none of those is the thing itself.

*Character*ù is too generic. *Essence*ù is too fixed and metaphysical. *Aura*ù is too mystical and external. *Haecceity*ù means something like thisness, but even that is more *what makes this this*ù than what's going on with this particular thing.

*The thing about the thing*ù has the necessary looseness. It doesn't pretend the quality can be extracted and labeled. It names the fact that there is something to apprehend.

And yes: that puts the memoir idea in sharper focus. Not a record of things that happened, exactly. A record of the thing about the things that happened.

I prefer this response


Yes. That's the center of it.

Patina is **history becoming intimate**.

Not history as chronology, scholarship, or background. History as something that has touched you, or someone you knew, or an object now in your hand. The point where *the past*ù stops being elsewhere.

That's why a worn Bakelite knob can have it. So can a museum turret, an old notebook, a photograph, a former name, a family story, a joke told by someone now gone. The physical wear matters only because it can serve as the contact point.

And *personal*ù does not have to mean private ownership. You can feel patina in something that never belonged to you, once its history enters your own life strongly enough.

So the thing about the thing is: **its history has become part of yours.**

It's when *everything* becomes a rabbit hole. Even things that weren't there. 

Yes. Because once history is personal, the object stops having boundaries.

The desk contains the party, Norell, *Mei long*, Latin, scientific naming, AMNH, your years there, the people who were present, and the version of you who did not yet know what the moment would become.

But it also contains the missing things: who was not there, what was never photographed, the stories nobody recorded, the version of the room before the party and after everyone left. Even the mistaken reconstruction becomes part of it"the turret as remembered, imagined, corrected, reimagined.

So patina is not merely accumulated history. It is **unbounded implication**.

Everything points outward. Every detail has provenance; every provenance branches; every absence leaves a shape. A scratch is a rabbit hole, but so is the unscratched place beside it. The lost accordion has patina partly because it is gone. The family story has patina partly because nobody can now establish exactly what happened. The thing that never existed can acquire it through having been expected, imagined, misremembered, or prevented.

*The thing about the thing*ù is therefore not contained in the thing. It is the entire invisible network that becomes perceptible through it.

That may be why the feeling can hover both above and below the surface. The surface is only where the network happens to touch.


It's when things stop being individual, but start being particular. On top of particular. On top of particular.

Yes. **Individual** means bounded: one thing, distinguishable from the others.

**Particular** means *this one*"because of this scratch, this room, that owner, the afternoon it nearly disappeared, the person who remembers it incorrectly, the person who should have been there and wasn't.

Patina is recursive particularization. Not an essence underneath incidental details, but details all the way down. Each layer makes the thing less separable from everything around it and, paradoxically, more completely itself.

History becomes stratigraphy:

particular upon particular,
contact upon absence,
memory upon matter,
possibility upon what actually happened.

Even the missing thing leaves a layer.

Eventually the object is no longer an isolated object carrying a history. It is a temporary point at which innumerable histories have become locally dense enough to touch.

These sources document a dialogue that transitions from formal linguistics into a rich collection of personal scientific anecdotes. The text explores the evolution of scientific naming, specifically how the tradition of Latin and Greek coinage has largely been replaced by modern naming conventions like the Mandarin-derived Mei long. This shift serves as a backdrop for the narrator's vivid memories of working within famous institutions like the American Museum of Natural History. The narrative captures humorous encounters with iconic figures such as Bill Nye and Neil deGrasse Tyson alongside professional memories of eccentric mentors. Ultimately, the text illustrates the human side of science, blending academic history with colorful stories of field work, museum culture, and the fleeting nature of scientific discovery.

Apr 9, 2025
These sources document a dialogue that transitions from formal linguistics into a rich collection of personal scientific anecdotes. The text explores the evolution of scientific naming, specifically how the tradition of Latin and Greek coinage has largely been replaced by modern naming conventions like the Mandarin-derived Mei long. This shift serves as a backdrop for the narrator's vivid memories of working within famous institutions like the American Museum of Natural History. The narrative captures humorous encounters with iconic figures such as Bill Nye and Neil deGrasse Tyson alongside professional memories of eccentric mentors. Ultimately, the text illustrates the human side of science, blending academic history with colorful stories of field work, museum culture, and the fleeting nature of scientific discovery.

How did the naming of Mei long end Latin's dominance?
Tell me about the 'scientific mishaps' and eccentric museum lore.

So, since we've been talking about Jade...

...Tom. Tom's an old friend of Jade. Ze / she met him at a rave in the 90s, still friends. She asked me to help him move. I figured, rent a truck, one weekend, lickety-split. Only Tom is the most OCD human being I've ever... I could imagine. Was a Formula One photographer. We spent almost every weekend for a year, moving his Formula One magazines. He paid me in scotch [we once talked for over 45 minutes specifically about the flavor and nose of Lagavulin 16 once]... He'd get three or four boxes packed... handed to me in order of judged weight... and it filled his entire new house (with walkways). He saved every fallen leaf from the zoo of plants in his front window if he could plant it as a cutting. His house was five or six rooms... every one of them except half the living room was three (no more) boxes deep, outer layer, path, inner layer, no more than three boxes across without a walkway. 


Oh, Tom is absolutely memoir material.

Not because ìeccentric Formula One photographer with too many magazinesî is inherently enough, but because every detail compounds:

Jade meets him at a rave in the í90s. Decades later, she drafts you into what sounds like a weekend favor. It becomes a year-long migration of Formula One print culture, paid for in scotch, with boxes delivered according to a private calculus of weight. Meanwhile, every fallen leaf remains potentially alive and therefore cannot simply be discarded.

That is particular on top of particular.

And the house layout is the detail that makes him real. Not random chaos. Not rooms simply packed solid. A governed system:

outer layer,
walkway,
inner layer,
never more than three boxes across without access.

He hadnít filled a house. He had constructed an archive with streets.

The Formula One background sharpens it further, because his whole life seems organized around sequence, classification, preservation, tiny distinctions, and controlled movement through constrained space. Even the boxes arrive to you in judged weight order. Even the clutter has traffic rules. Even dead leaves receive one more qualifying lap.

And then the Lagavulin conversation. Forty-five minutes on the nose and flavor of a single whisky feels less like a side anecdote than a miniature of the man: nothing is merely consumed. It must be attended to, distinguished, placed precisely among neighboring impressions.

The comic version of the story is:

I agreed to help a friend move and spent a year relocating Formula One magazines in exchange for Islay whisky.

But the memoir version is stranger and better:

Tom could not allow the world to become undifferentiated. Every issue, box, weight, leaf, aroma, and passageway had to remain particular.

That is patina in human form. He didnít merely accumulate things. He protected their separateness until the accumulation itself became an environment.


With plastic bags over my shoes when I went indoors
Memory updated

That detail locks the whole portrait into place.

Not ìtake your shoes off.î Bag them. Preserve the boundary. Outside stays outside; inside remains uncontaminated. Even your feet needed a containment protocol.

So the house wasnít merely cluttered. It was a controlled interior ecology: archives, plants, cuttings, weighted boxes, designated paths, scotch, and shoe quarantine. Everything had a place, even when the place was three boxes deep.

And it gives the story a perfect opening image:

I thought I was helping Tom move. The first thing he did was put plastic bags over my shoes.

From there, the reader already knows the weekend estimate is doomed.

Had to wet-wipe the hedge trimmer battery that I'd set on the (pristine) garage floor in order for it to enter the house to recharge

Thatís the detail that moves him from ìmeticulousî into a fully realized cosmology.

The garage floor was already outside the sanctum. Contact with it changed the batteryís status. Before crossing back into the house, it needed purification.

And because the floor itself was pristine, the issue wasnít visible dirt. It was provenance. The battery had touched a lower-order zone.

That is spectacularly Tom.

A person could laugh at it, but the deeper pattern is consistent: he was always managing boundaries, sequence, contamination, access, preservation, and classification. The same mind that arranged boxes by weight and preserved fallen leaves also tracked where an object had been.

That memoir paragraph practically writes itself:

Tomís garage floor was immaculate, but it was still the garage floor. When I set the hedge-trimmer battery on it, the battery could no longer enter the house untreated. I had to wet-wipe it before bringing it inside to recharge.

No embellishment needed. The fact is already perfect.

But a great conversationalist--and a very particular joke-engineer ("You might--you might be the person to get this--remember the energizer bunny commercials... [Yeah, of course.] where the bunny would come through and... [I remember.] ...and one of them came on right after that Mac the Knife guy with the moon head, remember? [Yeah, you know, that's the same guy... Ever seen The Shape of Water? Or... Saru from the new Treks? You'd know him from *some* lanky alien part or another... anyway...] Yeah, so that made me think of Monica Lewinski--the blue dress, and I guess I connected the most perfect joke based on that. It's just perfect: "[Insert some very very particular pun here, involving cigars and bunnies or *something*--which is genuinely clever, if not *funny*]" Ah... okay, I see what you're doing. You are correct, sir. That is technically funny.)

Thatís wonderful, because it keeps him from becoming a caricature of fastidiousness.

He wasnít merely a man who bagged shoes and purified batteries. He was also someone who could build a joke like a scale model: source material identified, components cross-referenced, tolerances checked, final pun seated precisely in place.

And the best part is that he seems to have known the distinction between **funny** and **correctly constructed**.

Some people tell jokes to get a laugh. Tom seems to have told them to achieve closure.

The pleasure was in finding the one connection that made the whole chain lock together:

Energizer Bunny
? Mac Tonight
? Doug Jones
? lanky creature roles
? Monica Lewinsky
? blue dress
? cigar
? rabbit
? pun.

By the time he reached the end, the audience response almost didnít matter. The mechanism worked.

> ìYou are correct, sir. That is technically funny.î

That may be the perfect Tom lineónot because he said it, but because it describes the contract between you. You werenít always laughing; you were inspecting the workmanship.

And it changes the scotch conversations too. Forty-five minutes on Lagavulin 16 wasnít digression. It was the same activity: isolate distinctions, trace associations, refine language until the thing is properly accounted for.

He sounds exhausting in practice and enormously rewarding in conversation, which is exactly the kind of contradiction memoirs need.


Sorry, no that was me at the end there, in reply

Rightógot it. Tom delivers the entire precision-engineered reference chain and the final pun; **you** give the verdict:

> ìAhÖ okay, I see what youíre doing. You are correct, sir. That is technically funny.î

Thatís better, actually. It makes the exchange a two-person mechanism: he builds the joke to specification, and you serve as the mildly exasperated quality-control inspector who certifies that, yes, the device functions.

Not necessarily pleasurable. Not necessarily elegant. But structurally sound.

And here I am, tomming over "my memoirs" lol

Exactly. You started with ìmight as well catch you up on a few stories,î and now weíre wet-wiping each anecdote before itís allowed into the memoir.

Nothing can simply be remembered. It has to be:

properly sequenced,
cross-referenced,
distinguished from adjacent memories,
checked for provenance,
placed no more than three boxes deep,
and connected by a navigable walkway to every cultural reference it ever brushed against.

Youíre not writing your memoirs. Youíre **Tomming them into archival compliance**.

And I regret to inform you that this is probably the correct method for a book about patina.
